  - id:
      orchestrator
    name:
      Bounded agent orchestration
    layer:
      runtime
    status:
      partial
    purpose:
      Run a finite reasoning/tool loop around a user request or workflow event, with explicit budgets, state and stop conditions.
    inputs:
      - Authenticated command or permitted trigger
      - Evidence packet, workflow version and allowed capabilities
    outputs:
      - Answer, proposed business change or scheduled continuation
      - Run trace and terminal/pending state
    dependencies:
      - policy
      - retrieval
      - model-router
      - output-validation
      - workflow-engine
    evidence:
      The dossier has extraction and answering runs plus adapter repairs. A complete durable multi-user production orchestrator is not evidenced.
    remaining:
      - Define request, run, step and workflow identifiers
      - Bound model calls, tool calls, time and cost
      - Persist transitions so workers can resume safely
      - Hold only work that depends on an unresolved decision; let independent work continue
    acceptance:
      - A timeout produces a visible run state rather than a false success
      - A pending payment approval does not stop unrelated inventory capture
      - A resumed run cannot execute a completed side effect again
    boundaries:
      Evaluation-run rules and production recovery rules are different contracts. Historical no-retry evaluation runs remain failed even if production could recover.
    sources:
      - E01
      - E03
      - H01
    sprint:
      S2–S3
    used_by:
      - device-apps
    search_terms:
      - Short-term memory
      - Planning
      - Multi-agent coordination
  - id:
      model-router
    name:
      Model gateway & cost routing
    layer:
      runtime
    status:
      proposed
    purpose:
      Make model providers replaceable through typed requests, capability checks and consistent outcomes. Use expensive reasoning only when measured task quality requires it.
    inputs:
      - Task type, sensitivity, budget and model capability requirements
    outputs:
      - Normalised model responses, usage and error categories
    dependencies:
      - secrets
      - observability
      - output-validation
    evidence:
      Several models were evaluated in the dossier. This shows comparative experiments, not a production routing layer or provider-independent quality.
    remaining:
      - Create provider adapters and normalise tool/JSON/streaming/error behaviour
      - Version prompts and model configuration per run
      - Benchmark routing on held-out episodes before enabling cheaper routes
      - Define fallback rules that respect residency, sensitivity and budget constraints
    acceptance:
      - Switching a provider preserves the application contract and reruns the release suite
      - Failures never appear as successful empty outputs
      - Cost is measured per completed correct business outcome, including retries and review
    boundaries:
      LLM-independent means substitutable providers with tested contracts, not equal performance or zero provider dependence.
    sources:
      - E01
      - E02
      - H01
    sprint:
      S3–S5
    used_by:
      - extraction
      - orchestrator
      - answers
      - browser
      - vision
      - voice
      - video
    search_terms:
      - Multi-agent coordination
  - id:
      output-validation
    name:
      Output, evidence & invariant checks
    layer:
      runtime
    status:
      partial
    purpose:
      Check structure, arithmetic, evidence links, business invariants and completion state before presenting or admitting a model result.
    inputs:
      - Model response and selected source packet
      - Domain invariants and expected output schema
    outputs:
      - Validated result or actionable failure/hold reason
    dependencies:
      - ledger
      - views
      - retrieval
    evidence:
      The dossier reports an error:null adapter fix, strict/material grading differences and a citation mismatch later repaired on two tested answers.
    remaining:
      - Make parse failure, unsupported evidence and business conflict distinct errors
      - Validate citations resolve to the exact supporting source version

      S2

      - device-apps

  - id:
      video
    name:
      Video understanding
    layer:
      intake
    status:
      proposed
    purpose:
      A kitchen clip shows water beneath a sink at a cited timestamp. Propose a maintenance task without inventing the cause or claiming repair completion.
    inputs:
      - Clip
      - Authenticated source scope and analysis question
    outputs:
      - Candidate observations with original-media/page/time references
      - Explicit processing status, model/version and coverage
    dependencies:
      - media
      - vision
      - voice
      - model-router
      - output-validation
    evidence:
      Observed in Hermes source: The opt-in video_analyze tool materializes a file, base64-encodes it and sends it to an auxiliary model. The inspected path does not itself extract frames. A separate YouTube skill reads transcripts. No SupaBiz implementation or runtime verification is evidenced.
    remaining:
      - Separate video analysis from transcript summarization; capability routing and bounded media handling.
      - Long-video segmentation, timestamp verification, audio alignment, cost limits and missing-coverage reporting. Frame sampling is a proposed SupaBiz extension, not an observed Hermes feature in this path.
    acceptance:
      - The encoded payload has a 50 MiB cap; actual raw capacity is lower because base64 expands data. A YouTube page is not a direct video file. Transcript-only analysis cannot establish visual events; sampled frames may miss short events.
      - Test source fidelity, denied access, ambiguous input and failure handling on held-out examples before release.
    boundaries:
      The encoded payload has a 50 MiB cap; actual raw capacity is lower because base64 expands data. A YouTube page is not a direct video file. Transcript-only analysis cannot establish visual events; sampled frames may miss short events.
    sources:
      - H01
    sprint:
      S3
    used_by:
      - extraction
    search_terms:
      - Multimodal perception
baseline_status_definitions:
  demonstrated:
    label:
      Demonstrated in dossier
    description:
      A narrow behaviour has a reported test or replay result. This does not establish production readiness.
  partial:
    label:
      Partial / gaps observed
    description:
      Some behaviour is reported working, with known failures or missing integration.
  reported:
    label:
      Founder-reported
    description:
      The founder reports access or functionality; implementation and reliability were not inspected.
  proposed:
    label:
      Proposed / verify build
    description:
      Required or recommended in the target design; implementation is not evidenced in this review.
  later:
    label:
      Later / conditional
    description:
      A deliberate later stage or optional dependency, gated by a demonstrated need.

      "implementation": "Avoid secrets or signed URLs in analytics/referrers and redact them from logs. Validate tenant/object ownership before issuing access.   "research_dossiers": [
    {
      "id": "P01-hermes",
      "name": "Hermes Agent",
      "category": "Personal agent runtime",
      "sections": {
        "What it is": "Hermes combines a conversational agent loop, model access, tools, channel adapters, persistent memory, skills and scheduled work. It is a working personal-agent application rather than only an SDK or a collection of prompts. Its security documentation describes a single-tenant personal-agent trust model. A messaging adapter accepting more than one authorized person does not establish independent enterprise roles or isolated business tenants.",
        "Runtime and tool flow": "A channel receives a message and resolves the authorized session. The agent composes its context, requests a model response and processes proposed tools. The selected turn-validation and turn-round modules validate tool calls and persist a tool-intent receipt before execution; failure to flush that receipt can stop execution. Tool results feed subsequent turns. These controls help reconstruct an interrupted turn, but they do not make every external operation transactional or exactly-once. A write to a remote application can succeed before its confirmation is safely recorded.",
        "Memory and extension": "Built-in memory uses MEMORY.md and USER.md, with a session snapshot included in the prompt. Updating those files persists information without necessarily rebuilding the current session's frozen prompt. The context engine provides separate assembly and provider-extension mechanisms. Skills are reusable procedures; selected skill-manager guards require context for mutation and support review. A skill's instructions and a platform's enforced access policy are different things. A complete extension review needs the tool implementation, its schema, its credential access and its filesystem/network effects.",
        "Browser, vision, voice and video": "The browser tool supports browser automation through agent-browser, accessible snapshots and element references, with local/remote browser connection options. Screenshots can be interpreted by a model rather than being treated as readable page text. The vision tool's video path materializes video bytes and sends encoded content to a compatible auxiliary model; the inspected path is opt-in and imposes an encoded size limit. It is not itself a universal local frame-analysis pipeline. Transcription dispatches to configured local or cloud backends. These modalities are separate tool/data paths, not proof that the main model directly perceives every file or video format.",
        "Channel and scheduling persistence": "The inspected WhatsApp bridge uses Baileys and an in-memory queue capped at 100 entries. Overflow removes an older item, and the messages endpoint removes queued messages before the downstream Python consumer has demonstrated durable processing. Full-history sync is disabled in this path. These are potential loss windows established by code inspection, not measured loss rates. Scheduled work separately uses occurrence identifiers and a delivery queue; an interrupted claimed send can be classified as unknown instead of automatically resent. That distinction addresses uncertainty about an external send rather than pretending every restart is safe to retry.",
        "Authority and operating environment": "Model-provider credentials, channel sessions, local files and tools are separate sources of authority. The security document treats authorized callers within the personal-agent deployment as trusted. A terminal sandbox alone does not constrain every other in-process tool; process/container permissions and network boundaries matter as well. Root code is MIT at this revision. Dependencies, model services and browser providers have their own operational requirements. No accounts were connected and no Hermes runtime or WhatsApp bridge was launched in this investigation.",
        "Experiments needed next": "Exercise a tool-intent persistence failure, restart after an external side effect, bridge overflow and a consumer crash after dequeue. Check how memory changes become visible in the current versus next session. Test browser state loss, a video above the encoded limit, unsupported audio, and a scheduled send whose response is lost. Those experiments would establish behavior under failure; the source alone cannot supply latency, reliability or cost percentages."

        "What it is": "Activepieces combines a visual workflow builder, a catalogue of integration packages called pieces, a server API, and workers that execute jobs. A piece supplies triggers, actions and configuration rather than being a complete workflow by itself. The builder connects these capabilities into a versioned flow. This distinction matters when reading the repository: a reusable connector, the workflow engine, and the hosted product are different units with different dependencies.",
        "Architecture and boundaries": "The selected Compose file separates APP and WORKER containers and supplies PostgreSQL and Redis. The API-side job broker owns BullMQ workers and dispatches jobs to execution workers; the execution worker selects a handler from a job-type registry. That registry covers flows, polling, webhooks, token refresh, validation, property resolution and single actions. AI and agent handlers are lazy-loaded. This means ordinary flow execution need not eagerly load the AI dependency graph. The public README's broad product claims should be read separately from this directly inspected execution machinery.",
        "Main execution flow": "A trigger or API request results in a typed job. The broker dequeues it, migrates and validates its schema, applies interceptors, and assigns it to an execution worker. It records ownership so a disconnected worker's in-flight jobs can be returned to the queue. Jobs have locks and attempt counts, and workers can extend their locks. The result is completed or failed through the broker. A separate run-metadata queue takes reported progress, checks the project/run identity, writes flow-run state, and coordinates relevant waitpoint/resume effects. Retrying a job is not in itself evidence that an external side effect occurs exactly once.",
        "Data and persistence": "PostgreSQL is the durable application database in the inspected deployment; Redis backs queues and coordination. Run-metadata handling takes a distributed lock and uses project-scoped keys. It checks for deleted flows and mismatched project ownership before updating records. The connection service stores encrypted values together with platform/project associations and exposes versions that omit sensitive values. Cache directories are mounted into both container roles. Backups would need to account for database state and operational queue state; recovery consistency was not exercised here.",
        "Authentication and trust": "Connection retrieval includes a project-membership condition, then decrypts and refreshes the credential as needed. The inspected return path removes refresh tokens and client secrets. The response-serialization ownership guard rejects a project mismatch on supported payload shapes. That guard is one layer, not a complete audit of every request, role, worker, or nested payload. Code actions and integration packages create executable trust boundaries that require separate sandbox analysis. The presence of encryption calls establishes a code path, not that deployment keys are correctly configured or protected.",
        "Deployment and extension": "The repository is a TypeScript monorepo; integration pieces are separately packaged.         "What it is": "Nango is a backend for connecting a product or agent to external APIs. Its abstractions include accounts, environments, integration configurations, end users, connections, connection sessions and execution jobs. It handles more than an OAuth login screen: the repository includes credential lifecycle, API access, sync execution, records and agent-facing MCP surfaces. A connection identifies an authorized relationship to a provider; it is not the same as an application user or an arbitrary permission to perform business actions.",
        "Architecture and main flow": "A client obtains a connection session for selected integrations and an end user. The server validates referenced integrations and stores the session in a transaction. A completed provider authentication creates or updates a connection. Subsequent server operations resolve the environment and integration/connection identifiers before obtaining credentials or metadata. Scheduled or requested sync tasks enter a separate jobs path, start an integration script, carry heartbeat and execution limits, and finish through success/error handlers. The MCP agent-session server registers tools and delegates tool calls through a common execution wrapper. These are separate boundaries, not one prompt with every API credential attached.",
        "Data and credentials": "The connection service contains transactional creation and update paths and tracks refresh success, failure, attempts and exhaustion. The encryption manager transforms credentials into an encrypted payload with IV and authentication tag when a key is configured. Crucially, its inspected shouldEncrypt check is conditional on a nonempty key; without one, encryptConnection returns the connection unchanged. This is a configuration boundary worth remembering, not evidence that the hosted service stores plaintext. The selected Compose file passes NANGO_ENCRYPTION_KEY and database settings explicitly and includes PostgreSQL and Redis.",
        "Authorization": "The authorization middleware evaluates required scopes against a resolved principal and returns forbidden when none match. The public connection endpoint separately tests the read_credentials scope. Requesting refresh-token or forced-refresh operations without that capability is rejected, and the response can instead contain connection metadata without credentials. Environment scoping and credential access are therefore observable concepts in the source. Complete end-user isolation, all proxy destinations and every MCP grant remain outside this bounded inspection.",
        "Deployment and extension": "The TypeScript monorepo separates server, shared services, jobs, runner SDK, records, persistence and other packages. Provider configuration and user-authored integration scripts are extension surfaces. A complete product deployment can involve more services than a minimal authentication-only setup. Sync script isolation, outbound policy, migration and key-rotation behavior need their own traced tests.",
        "Licensing and source boundary": "The collected root LICENSE is the controlling source document, with Elastic License 2.0 terms rather than a blanket MIT assumption.       "id": "P04-docling",
      "name": "Docling",
      "category": "Document parsing and conversion",
      "sections": {
        "What it is": "Docling is a document-processing library. Its central operation converts an input document into a structured document result that can be exported or consumed by downstream applications. It handles document structure as well as text: page layout, reading order, tables and optional enrichments. Parsing a supplier invoice or a court document does not by itself establish the business meaning of a number or date. The output is a structured interpretation of the source, with processing status and errors.",
        "Architecture": "DocumentConverter is the public entry point. Format-option classes associate formats with backends and pipelines. Its allowed_formats setting constrains accepted formats, and pipeline construction is cached using pipeline options. Backends supply format-specific access; pipelines build, assemble and optionally enrich a result. The standard PDF pipeline uses threaded stages and bounded queues around model-based processing. This separates decoding a source format from choosing OCR/layout/table models and from assembling a final document.",
        "Main execution flow": "The converter accepts a source, identifies or checks its format, selects a configured backend and obtains the corresponding pipeline. BasePipeline.execute creates a ConversionResult, performs build, assemble and enrich phases, determines status and releases resources in a finally block. A particularly useful inspected rule changes SUCCESS to PARTIAL_SUCCESS whenever errors remain. The PDF pipeline has explicit processing success/failure counts and assembly through a reading-order model. Consumers must inspect status and errors instead of treating a nonempty text export as complete coverage.",
        "State, models and output": "ConversionResult is per-document processing state; it is not a business database. Model artifacts can live in a configured artifacts directory and pipeline instances can be reused. The pipeline options expose OCR choices and table-processing choices. Optional image-description/API paths accept remote-service configuration; remote processing must be deliberately understood when selecting those options. The public library can be used locally, but local Python execution alone does not establish that every enabled model, download or optional service is offline.",
        "Authentication and deployment": "The core converter is a Python library, not a multi-tenant identity service. A server wrapping it must control file access, URLs, input limits, job scheduling, authorization and output retention. Optional dependencies and model downloads affect installation and hardware requirements. The inspected configuration supports document timeouts and error reporting; large, malformed or hostile inputs still need bounded execution in any deployment. No OCR model, GPU pipeline, external API or server was run in this research.",
        "Extension points and licensing": "Format options and backend/pipeline interfaces are the principal extension seams. An application can choose another OCR option, customize enrichment or add a format-specific backend without redesigning all downstream consumers. Root code is MIT-licensed, while model weights and dependencies retain their own terms. The repository and pyproject identify a broad ecosystem; this investigation did not verify all optional backends or the terms of every model artifact.",
        "What remains to learn": "A useful runtime study would collect representative scanned, digital and mixed-layout documents, including rotated pages, multi-page tables, handwriting and deliberately unreadable regions. Compare extracted fields against human-labelled page regions, separately measuring reading order, table structure and missing coverage. Exercise a timeout and a failed page to verify partial-success propagation into exported formats. Measure CPU/GPU memory, model initialization and steady-state latency separately. No accuracy percentage follows from this source inspection."

      "id": "P05-temporal",
      "name": "Temporal",
      "category": "Durable workflow infrastructure",
      "sections": {
        "What it is": "Temporal separates durable workflow coordination from the application workers that execute workflow and activity code. A workflow describes progression and waiting; activities perform work that can involve external systems. The server stores execution history and schedules tasks. This is an infrastructure service with SDKs, not a visual integration catalogue or a language-model agent. Durable recovery of orchestration does not make every external effect exactly-once.",
        "Server architecture": "The inspected Go server exposes a frontend workflow API, history handlers and a matching engine. StartWorkflowExecution validates and prepares the request, resolves the namespace identifier, then delegates to the history service. The matching engine deals with task-queue partitions, sticky queues and worker polling. The persistence interfaces separately describe execution state, history events, transactions and conditional-write failures. Application worker code and SDK replay behavior are a separate side of the architecture; this pass inspected server paths and the official workflow documentation.",
        "Execution flow": "A client requests a workflow start with identifiers, timeouts and retry-related configuration. Frontend validation passes the request into history handling. Workflow tasks and activity tasks are dispatched via matching to workers polling the relevant queues. Workers return completions, failures or cancellations; history handlers expose corresponding operations and activity heartbeats. A waiting workflow need not keep a request handler alive. Progress is represented by durable execution state and history rather than relying on an in-memory agent conversation.",
        "Persistence and recovery": "The persistence contract distinguishes mutable execution state from history event batches and records transaction relationships. Task metadata includes namespace, workflow/run identifiers, scheduled event identity, expiry and version directives. Conditional failures and timeouts are represented explicitly. Official workflow documentation explains deterministic replay: workflow code reconstructs state from history. External I/O belongs in activities, whose retry and timeout semantics must be designed around the external service. Application-side idempotency or reconciliation is still needed when a remote effect succeeds but its acknowledgement is lost.",
        "Authentication and deployment": "Namespaces organize execution, but a namespace name is not by itself an authorization boundary. The server's authentication/authorization configuration was not traced in this pass. Deployment includes the Temporal server, a supported persistence backend, appropriate visibility storage/configuration, and application workers running the chosen SDK. Hosted Temporal Cloud and self-hosted Temporal have different operational responsibilities. Server upgrades, workflow code changes, history growth, worker versioning and backup consistency are material areas for a deployment review.",
        "Extension and licensing": "Applications extend the system through workflows, activities, worker task queues, signals/updates and SDK integrations rather than by forking the Go server. Root server code is MIT-licensed. Language SDKs and infrastructure dependencies must be inspected at their own pinned versions before asserting compatibility. A design discussion can use Temporal's concepts without deciding to operate a production cluster.",
        "Follow-up study": "Trace one workflow through start, activity scheduling, failure, retry, signal, cancellation and completion using a pinned SDK. Restart workers while a timer is outstanding, then interrupt an activity after a stub service has recorded its side effect. Inspect history and the external service ledger independently. A code-change replay test is needed before assuming an in-flight workflow survives a changed implementation. Throughput, recovery time and operational cost remain unmeasured."
      },

      "id": "P06-browser-use",
      "name": "Browser Use",
      "category": "Browser agent and automation library",
      "sections": {
        "What it is": "Browser Use is a Python system that lets a model operate a browser through structured observations and registered actions. It includes an agent loop as well as browser/session machinery. The open-source library and the separately offered cloud browser/API are distinct surfaces. A browser task can read content, navigate forms, download files or submit changes, but completion of a model-generated plan is not independent proof that a remote business transaction succeeded.",
        "Architecture and main flow": "Agent.step prepares browser context, clears stale step outputs, obtains the next model action, executes actions, post-processes results, and finalizes in all cases. Errors enter a central recovery path. BrowserSession manages the browser/CDP connection, targets, tabs and an event bus. The tools registry exposes navigation, element-index or coordinate clicks, input, uploads, extraction, scrolling, screenshots, PDF saving, file operations and completion. DOM services and screenshots provide different observation channels; the model does not have unrestricted knowledge of all site content.",
        "State and observation": "The agent keeps a task history and per-step results. Browser state includes tabs, cookies and optionally persisted storage state. The session offers explicit storage-state export and browser-profile selection. Consequently browser identity is a valuable retained capability, not just a convenience file. A source snapshot and the later screenshot can disagree after navigation or page mutation; the loop must observe again. The inspected implementation clears prior results before an LLM call so a timed-out step does not accidentally retain the previous step's outputs.",
        "Authentication and boundaries": "The API accepts sensitive-data mappings and domain restrictions. The agent warns when sensitive values are supplied without allowed domains, and checks whether domain-specific credential patterns are covered. Those checks are useful evidence of configuration awareness, not proof that every browser/network path is isolated. Session cookies, downloads, filesystem actions and arbitrary page evaluation create separate authority surfaces. Full prompt-injection resistance, redirect behavior, cross-origin policy and download confinement require targeted tests.",
        "Deployment and extension": "A developer supplies a compatible model, browser profile/session and a task, or integrates the tools into another harness. Registered actions can be extended or excluded, and structured final outputs can be configured. Local browser execution and hosted browser operation require different credentials and operational assumptions. The Python package, browser binary and model provider must be versioned separately. No browser was launched by this investigation and no real authenticated account was connected.",
        "Licensing and practical limits": "The root library license is MIT; this does not describe commercial cloud service terms or every browser/model dependency. Source includes recovery and timeout machinery, but success against changing websites, MFA, dynamic elements and inaccessible controls is unmeasured here. An action's success response can mean the browser interaction completed, while the site's application rejected the request afterward. Verification should inspect resulting page/application state.",
        "Next experiments": "Use a controlled site containing delayed forms, reordered elements, redirects, downloads and a fake authenticated session. Compare DOM-only operation with screenshot fallback. Interrupt the browser connection mid-step and verify honest failure and reconnect behavior. Test that an unapproved destination cannot receive credential substitutions. Measure final-task correctness, interaction count and latency on fixed pages rather than relying on promotional speed comparisons."

      "id": "P07-langfuse",
      "name": "Langfuse",
      "category": "Agent observability and evaluation platform",
      "sections": {
        "What it is": "Langfuse records and analyzes LLM application activity: traces, observations, model calls, scores, datasets, experiments and prompts. It can help answer what a run did and how a version scored. An observability system records evidence supplied to it; it does not independently establish that a domain action was correct. An evaluator's score remains dependent on the evaluator, dataset and rubric.",
        "Architecture": "The inspected deployment separates a web/API service and a worker and includes PostgreSQL, ClickHouse, Redis and S3-compatible object storage through MinIO. PostgreSQL schemas contain identities, organizations/projects, API keys, memberships, datasets, prompts and job configuration. ClickHouse is used in the ingestion service for observation/trace/score records. Redis and background workers support asynchronous processing; object storage supports ingestion/media paths. This is a multi-service data system, not just a browser charting library.",
        "Trace-to-evaluation flow": "Instrumented application events reach ingestion. The inspected OpenTelemetry worker converts resource spans into event inputs, enriches event records, optionally processes media, and separately schedules observation evaluations and direct event-table writes. Evaluation configuration retrieval can fail without preventing every other processing step. IngestionService dispatches different event types and merges trace/observation updates before persistence. Its handling of stored and incoming records matters when events arrive out of order. A queued evaluation and a persisted event are related but independently failing operations.",
        "Storage and governance": "Trace content can contain prompts, tool arguments, returned text and media references; it can therefore be as sensitive as the application being observed. Schema identity, trace IDs and project IDs must remain consistent through ingestion and UI reads. Dataset items and runs provide test organization, while prompt versions and scores provide change/evaluation metadata. Retention, deletion across stores, masking and large-field overflow are additional paths that were located but not exhaustively audited.",
        "Authorization": "The inspected authorize function scopes policies to a tenant and resource, prefers an exact-resource match over a wildcard, and denies when there is no matching action grant or a matching deny. Wildcards are not accepted as concrete authorization targets. The schema also distinguishes organizations, projects, memberships and API keys. This establishes an explicit policy model; it does not prove all API/UI/export paths invoke it correctly.",
        "Deployment, extensions and licensing": "Applications integrate through supported instrumentation/API paths; operators configure evaluators, prompt workflows and storage. Self-hosting requires managing multiple durable stores and background processing rather than just installing an SDK. The root license uses MIT for code outside designated enterprise directories and preserves separate third-party terms. The pinned deployment currently references version-4 images; older version-3 deployment assumptions should not be silently reused.",
        "Follow-up work": "Trace a redacted test application through SDK batching, ingestion acknowledgement, worker processing and UI visibility. Force out-of-order events, duplicate uploads, a stopped worker and unavailable object storage. Test masking before data leaves the application and deletion across all retained stores. For evaluation, use human-labelled outcomes and report disagreement between model judges and ground truth. Neither instrumenting a run nor receiving a high score proves the original action happened correctly."

        "Architecture": "The runtime separates agent identity and conversation state from the computer on which tools execute. The README describes local operation and Letta Cloud, which retains agent memory, identity and conversations while execution can occur on different machines. Public package exports include App Server client/protocol, memory-confinement, MCP and channel surfaces. Memory, approvals and server transport are independent subsystems. The hosted Cloud backend is described by upstream documentation; its full production implementation was not inspected here.",
        "Memory flow and persistence": "MemFS stores agent memory in filesystem structures with Git-backed history. The inspected memory-filesystem module resolves per-agent/scoped roots, creates directories, stamps memory-enabled tags on agent creation and materializes local memory checkouts. Memory-runtime resolves whether MemFS is enabled from persisted settings with an in-memory fallback. This makes memory an explicit artifact with lifecycle and history. It does not establish that a model-written memory statement is factually correct, that every Git conflict is automatically resolved, or that all changes have human approval.",
        "Approval and execution flow": "Resume handling reads message history to reconstruct pending tool approvals and recognizes completed tool-call IDs.         "Server, authentication and channels": "The App Server CLI parses a WebSocket listener and capability-token or signed-bearer-token configuration, including issuer, audience and clock-skew options.         "Deployment, extension and license": "The current package can run interactively or serve remote clients. Skills, hooks, model connections, channels and mods are extension surfaces.       "id": "P09-security-audit",

      "category": "Security research workflow",
      "sections": {
        "What it is": "This repository packages a procedure for a coding agent to investigate a codebase and retain verified findings. Its main product is an audit workflow plus structured artifacts and validators. It is not an always-on firewall, an identity provider or a certificate that a target is secure. The discovery model, target source and execution environment remain external dependencies.",
        "Architecture and flow": "The documented workflow starts by mapping architecture, trust boundaries and input surfaces into a coverage ledger. Hunting tasks investigate ledger units. Candidate findings then go to fresh verifiers who attempt to disprove them. Findings are recorded under confirmed, needs_validation or rejected verdicts. Another verification step checks final source claims before reports are generated from the structured records. Keeping a rejected candidate is useful: it retains why a suspicion failed instead of turning every deviation from a checklist into a vulnerability.",
        "Artifacts and state": "The coverage ledger tracks investigated units and gaps; findings.json records stable fingerprints, traces, evidence, prerequisites and outcomes. The report schema requires explicit source files/lines and scopes for trace entries. A stable fingerprint is meant to survive a change in verdict. Reports are derived views of this retained state. Repeated audits can revisit changed source and unresolved gaps, but a ledger's existence is not proof every attack path has been covered.",
        "Validator mechanics": "The inspected Node validator interprets the repository's supported schema keywords and adds finding-specific checks. It bounds input size, nesting, array items and error output, uses fatal UTF-8 decoding and sanitizes diagnostics. This validates structure and consistency of submitted records. It cannot decide whether an exploit actually occurred merely because an evidence paragraph is well-formed. Runtime evidence and independent source checking are separate requirements.",
        "Execution and trust": "The workflow requires an agent with tool use and separate verifier contexts, plus an OS-enforced sandbox for target-controlled code. Documentation calls for controlled networking, resource limits, a sanitized environment and restricted writes; when these are absent, an unresolved lead remains needs_validation. Target code, tests and instructions can be adversarial inputs to the auditor. The investigation should preserve that distinction even when a repository contains its own audit instructions.",
        "Extension, license and use boundaries": "Attack-class guides cover AI tools, auth, client-side behavior, cloud, supply chain, data isolation and desktop/mobile boundaries. The guides can be selected according to a target's actual surfaces. Root license is MIT. We read the workflow, report schema and validator as source material; we did not activate the skill, launch an audit fleet, run target code or claim a security assessment of any project in this library.",
        "Next validation": "Test the validators with both valid and deliberately malformed records, then conduct a narrowly scoped audit against a known test fixture containing a confirmed flaw and a plausible false positive. Check that the verifier disproves the false positive and preserves the real evidence. Evaluate audit coverage and reproducibility separately from the number of findings. No discovery-rate or safety percentage has been established here."

      "id": "P10-skills",
      "name": "Vercel Skills / Find Skills",
      "category": "Capability discovery and distribution",
      "sections": {
        "What it is": "The skills CLI discovers and distributes reusable agent instruction packages. A skill typically combines a SKILL.md description with supporting scripts or reference files. Find Skills is the discovery capability within this ecosystem. Installing a skill makes material available to an agent client; it does not create an OS sandbox, grant application permissions, or prove that the instructions are useful or trustworthy.",
        "Architecture and flow": "The CLI parses a source reference, locates candidate skill directories, reads skill metadata and installs selected packages for chosen agent environments. The find command sends a query to a search API, defaulting to skills.sh, and formats matching names, sources and popularity metadata. The source parser supports different repository/URL/local forms. Discovery parses frontmatter and searches bounded directory structures. Installation integrates with agent-specific locations and supports shared symlinks or copies.",
        "State and metadata": "The implementation references local lock data when discovering and matching installed skills. The installed files, canonical source, selected skill and update state are the relevant persistent artifacts. Symlinks reduce duplicate copies but also mean multiple agent clients can see the same changed content. Copy installs have separate update drift. A pinned version or hash is necessary to distinguish what was reviewed from what a later update supplies.",
        "Trust and authentication": "The inspected search and skill parsers sanitize displayed metadata and strip terminal control content. Safe-subpath checks help constrain discovery. The README describes using existing Git authentication for private sources. These address particular boundaries; they do not establish that downloaded instructions or scripts are safe to execute. A skill's prose is content supplied to an agent, and any executable helpers retain the agent's actual tool permissions. Search ranking and installation counts are discovery signals, not verification.",
        "Deployment and extension": "The CLI runs in the development/agent environment and supports many clients through location mappings. A publisher can add a repository containing skills and metadata rather than host a new application service.         "Architecture": "The installed CLI supplies native globalThis.ego bindings. The repository builds an SDK bundle plus a matching usage guide and skill files.         "Observe and act flow": "A snapshot request invokes the native snapshot operation, compacts the returned result and registers element references in a reference map. snapshotText requests full-page text with action marks and stable locator information by default.         "State and identity": "Browser logins, cookies and profile data belong to the installed browser.         "What is actually public": "The public repository is a client-plugin distribution for connecting an agent to Zapier MCP. It is not the source of Zapier's hosted integration engine. Consequently this dossier combines inspected client-side files with explicitly attributed official service documentation.         "Connection and execution flow": "Official connection documentation describes Streamable HTTP at the shared MCP connect endpoint. A client can use OAuth sign-in or a server token, then discover available tools and invoke actions against connected applications. OAuth refresh behavior belongs to the client/server authorization exchange; application credentials are managed through Zapier connections. A server token is authority to call the configured service, even if the underlying application's password is never shown to the language model. The public plugin configuration points the agent toward this remote boundary.",
        "Tool selection and authorization": "Official tool documentation distinguishes agentic discovery from a managed set of tools. Discovery/setup capabilities can find actions and enable appropriate application tools; execution capabilities then perform the selected reads or writes. The documented auto-provisioning path uses the user's own connections and excludes connections merely shared by another user. This is an account/service rule described by Zapier, not something enforced in the small public plugin repository. Availability of additional workflow or custom-code capabilities may depend on account and rollout status.",
        "State, cost and failure": "Connections and configured MCP access live in the hosted service. The client retains its own authentication/session material according to its implementation. The usage page reviewed for this dossier describes two tasks per successful external application call, with failed calls and discovery/setup excluded; plan limits can prevent additional calls. This is a dated service-policy observation, not a permanent pricing guarantee. The available source does not establish a durable side-effect receipt protocol, idempotency across application APIs or recovery after a response is lost.",
        "Extension and licensing boundary": "A client can select/configure exposed tools and use supported app connections. The repository's MIT license applies to the public plugin material; it is not a license to self-host the proprietary backend. For an engineering review, track the plugin revision separately from live service documentation and account-specific capability. Hosted behavior can change without a commit to this plugin repository.",
        "What a later test should establish": "Use a disposable app connection to inspect discovery, managed-tool restrictions, token revocation, a refused action and an exhausted usage allowance.       "id": "P13-arena",
      "name": "Arena Skill",
      "category": "Candidate generation and tournament evaluation",
      "sections": {
        "What it is": "Arena turns one task into a tournament of alternative answers. A coordinating agent supplies a task brief, competitors receive different strategy cards, and pairwise attacks, defenses and judging reduce the field to one answer. The Python component is a bookkeeping state machine; it does not itself contain the model service or guarantee that the selected answer is correct.",
        "Architecture and main flow": "The skill describes plan, init, next, prompts, check, collect, advance and winner commands. Initial competitors write solutions; each match then has two attacks, two defenses and one judge. The coordinator is instructed not to compete or judge. A separate final comparison can compare the champion with a prior baseline. Strategy combinations and a seed vary candidate approaches and pairings. A fixed seed makes that assignment reproducible, not the model's complete reasoning or output.",
        "Persistent state": "A run lives under .arena with an arena.json state file and separate briefs, solutions, attacks and verdicts. LATEST points to the active run. State survives the coordinating agent losing conversational context. The next command derives the next phase from disk, while check can identify missing outputs. This is a useful distinction between orchestration progress and transient chat memory. Shared-file concurrency, atomicity and adversarial output handling still require focused validation.",
        "Cost and evaluation": "The default is 100 competitors; quick mode uses 16. The source defines five model calls per match after the initial candidate generation, so a full 100-candidate elimination tournament entails 595 calls before an optional baseline judge. That is a plan count, not a measured token or currency cost. Rubric weights cover correctness, completeness, specificity, robustness and clarity. Pairwise judging may still reward convincing errors or share correlated blind spots. The winner is the winner of this procedure, not a proof of truth.",
        "Execution environment and permissions": "The orchestrator's agent system actually launches workers and grants their tools. The Python CLI uses standard-library filesystem/JSON state. It is not an execution sandbox or credential broker. Task briefs must carry sufficient context because child agents do not automatically receive the entire parent conversation. No tournament, subagent work or model calls were launched while reviewing this repository.",
        "License and next tests": "The root license is MIT. Tests exist for bracket mechanics and consistency with rubric weights; they were inspected as source, not run. A useful validation would start with known-answer tasks and compare a single attempt, a small diverse set and the full bracket at equal cost. Track judge errors, final factual accuracy, missing outputs and variation across seeds. Test resumption after every phase boundary independently of model quality."
      },

        "Architecture and task flow": "The source separates Electron/main-process services, renderer-facing interfaces, agent connection adapters and execution-host routing.         "Workspace state and lifecycle": "Git worktrees provide separate checkouts for concurrent tasks. Metadata tracks creation provenance, workspace layout, push targets and ownership.         "Agent and browser boundaries": "The ACP adapter handles an external agent connection, while other launch modes use terminals. Browser route/partition bindings have their own store. These claims are partly architectural documentation, corroborated here by the engine/document crate surfaces and automation module boundaries; the whole command catalogue and renderer were not audited.",
        "Automation flow": "An automation client reaches a headless backend or a bridge to a running app. The automation crate exposes RPC, server, file handling, budgets, security and workspace modules. Missing authority fails closed. Request paths are treated as untrusted and resolved beneath directory handles. This is more specific than simply giving a model a path string and assuming it stays inside a folder.",
        "State, files and security": "The native format is documented as a .pcraft document with a manifest and image/tile content. The inspected automation security module bounds JSON request size, simultaneous TCP connections, batch length and I/O timeouts. It requires authentication as the first TCP request and imposes a separate authentication deadline. Tokens are generated from OS randomness and compared without an early mismatch exit. These are concrete local-control mechanisms, not a full security certification.",
        "Deployment and extension": "The documented desktop uses egui/eframe with wgpu; browser builds use WebAssembly. Headless CLI and MCP avoid forcing every automation through screen coordinates. We did not build Rust targets, launch the application or measure output parity.",
        "Limitations and next tests": "Do not equate an extensive command list with Photoshop parity.       "category": "Native image editor with file-based automation",
      "sections": {
        "What it is": "Compositor is a native macOS image editor organized around layered compositing, adjustments and post-processing. The inspected project is Swift/Xcode based. Its agent integration is particularly concrete: another program can write the editor's document package, and the open app can observe those changes. That mechanism differs from an agent remotely clicking every GUI control and from a standalone hosted image-generation service.",
        "Architecture and interaction flow": "EditorSession coordinates editing state and project interaction; document-history code manages undo/history. ProjectStore serializes and loads document packages. ProjectController's external-change path watches for changes, reloads suitable updates and handles conflict with unsaved user edits. Rendering modules are separate in the repository, although detailed GPU algorithms were not inspected here. An automated file write and a successful application reload are two events that need separate verification.",
        "File contract and persistence": "A .comp package contains a JSON manifest plus referenced image data. Layer identifiers, ordering, transforms, blend modes, masks and document identity form the contract. The writer guide says to place image files first and then atomically replace the manifest; it also explains that reloading clears undo and that unsaved user work needs a choice rather than silent replacement. ProjectStore validates format/version, dimensions, layer structure and referenced images before accepting a document, and saves the package through coordinated atomic replacement.",
        "Documentation versus current code": "The writer guide gives an 8-bit PNG-oriented external-writing contract. The selected ProjectStore code additionally accepts version-gated higher-depth/HDR cases, including 16-bit image handling and OpenEXR for newer format versions. Therefore the simple guide is not a complete specification of every current saved document. An external writer should choose and validate a supported subset rather than assuming the guide's examples cover all internal possibilities. The guide also documents a change-detection caveat for an image rewritten to exactly the same byte size without a manifest change.",
        "Authority and deployment": "The automation boundary is local filesystem access to the selected project package. Any program allowed to write that package can affect its content; this is not a user/tenant authorization service. Package validation protects file interpretation but does not authenticate the identity of the writer. The README currently requires recent macOS/Xcode and Apple silicon. Those platform requirements are documented, not tested on this machine. Root code is MIT; bundled dependencies and release-signing infrastructure are separate.",
        "Follow-up experiments": "Create a disposable document, change a layer through the package format, and verify the live reload while preserving the document ID. Test a missing image, invalid layer graph, same-size replacement, unsaved user edit, interrupted save and a stale writer overwriting a newer version. Compare exported pixels with the live canvas. Validate version handling for higher-depth images before adopting assumptions from the simplified guide. No application was installed or launched here."

      "id": "P20-slopmonster",
      "name": "SlopMonster",
      "category": "Prose linting and optional model rewrite",
      "sections": {
        "What it is": "SlopMonster is a text-quality toolkit with a deterministic prose linter and an optional rewrite workflow using an installed model CLI. Its central value is an explicit set of editorial patterns that can be inspected and rerun. It is not an AI-authorship detector, a fact checker or a proof that a piece of writing is good. A clean result means the configured rules did not flag the normalized input.",
        "Input-to-report flow": "The deslop Python script accepts text and normalizes supported document forms before applying pattern checks. Its HTML path removes markup, including script/style content, without rendering a page or evaluating CSS. Its Markdown handling removes selected non-prose structures such as code and image syntax. It then checks vocabulary, phrases, punctuation, rhythm and conversion/social-proof patterns. The scoring logic starts from five and deducts for categories with findings, rather than interpreting each match as a calibrated probability.",
        "Output and failure semantics": "The tool produces a report useful to a person or another coding assistant. Empty input is a failure case, and a score below the clean threshold can produce a failing exit status for automation. This makes a lint gate possible, but also creates false-positive and coverage questions. For example, domain terminology, quoted text and writing in another language may not fit the rules. Stripping markup can change what the analyzer sees relative to what a reader sees on a rendered page.",
        "Optional rewrite path": "The cleanse shell script delegates rewriting to an available model CLI from a different family according to its routing rules. It includes timeout handling and distinct failures for unavailable clients. The expected response separates rewritten body from notes using a sentinel. If that sentinel is absent, the script can warn and retain broader output. This is an output-format convention dependent on another program and model, not a structured-response guarantee enforced by the Python linter.",
        "State, authority and dependencies": "The linting path can operate over local text without an external model. Rewriting inherits the installed CLI's authentication, data handling, available permissions and potential usage costs. A read-only option in one client invocation does not establish a universal sandbox for all supported clients. There is no inspected durable workflow database or enterprise authorization service. Root material is MIT; model-provider access and CLI versions remain separate dependencies.",
        "Extension and verification": "Rules can be extended in the Python implementation and checked against examples, while the rewrite prompt/routing can be changed separately. The repository includes a test file, but target tests were not executed in this investigation. A useful validation set would pair acceptable prose with intentionally flagged prose, include citations/code/HTML, and compare rewrite factual preservation sentence by sentence. A higher lint score must not be used as a substitute for that editorial review."

    {
      "id": "P21-openhands",
      "name": "OpenHands / Agent Canvas",
      "category": "Agent interface and separate execution SDK",
      "sections": {
        "Project identity at this revision": "The pinned OpenHands main repository presents Agent Canvas: a React/TypeScript interface for conversations, files, terminals, browser views and automation. Older descriptions of OpenHands as a single Python coding-agent repository are insufficient for this revision. The execution machinery belongs to separate Agent Server/SDK components. This dossier therefore captures the main UI repository and selected files from OpenHands/software-agent-sdk as two independently pinned sources.",
        "Architecture and interaction flow": "Canvas selects a backend, connects to its API, creates or attaches to a conversation and renders events and workspace views. The Agent Server owns execution and conversation lifecycle. In the selected conversation service, a request resolves agent settings and a launch source, scopes supplied secrets, registers tool definitions, optionally plans a worktree and constructs the launched agent/workspace. Client-defined JSON tool schemas are distinct from importing Python tool modules. Plugins are described as loading lazily when the conversation first runs. The automation service is another component; its complete implementation was not inspected.",
        "State and persistence": "Canvas stores backend definitions in browser localStorage and keeps the active selection primarily tab-scoped in sessionStorage, with a local fallback. The backend object includes its API-key field, so browser-origin security is relevant to that storage design. ConversationService maintains conversation lifecycle and persisted event access separately from the UI. Its presence does not by itself prove that every external command can be resumed without duplication. Git worktrees separate checkout state, while execution isolation depends on how the server and runtime are actually launched.",
        "Authentication and trust boundaries": "The inspected UI auth helper uses X-Session-API-Key for local backends and bearer authentication for applicable cloud configurations; cookie mode is handled separately. The SDK dependency checks session keys against configuration, and its selected check bypasses that requirement when no keys are configured. That observation is limited to this function, not a conclusion about every route or deployment. Backend address, allowed origins, browser storage, server credentials and runtime/container permissions form distinct boundaries.",
        "Deployment and extension": "Self-hosting documentation describes local operation and alternative runtime configurations, including per-conversation Docker execution. Binding a server beyond loopback changes exposure, and containers do not automatically protect all host-side automation or shared workspace state. Extension points include tools, agent launch configuration, plugins/skills and backend integration. Main and SDK root licenses are MIT at the recorded revisions; deployment services and dependencies must be checked separately. No OpenHands containers, servers or agents were started here.",
        "What the source establishes and leaves open": "The code establishes a separation between interface, server lifecycle and execution workspace, plus concrete client-side auth and persistence choices. It does not establish a fully audited multi-tenant system, safe arbitrary tool imports or universal recovery semantics. A controlled follow-up should create a conversation on a disposable backend, disconnect/reconnect the UI, interrupt execution, rotate a session key and compare worktree versus container boundaries. Verify host mounts and secret propagation before interpreting a Docker option as complete isolation."
